# Research ledger

Every fact in the app traces to a row here. Researcher agents collected each claim with a verbatim quote from an allowed source; independent fact-checker agents re-opened the source and tried to refute it. Strict policy: only claims a checker confirmed or corrected are usable, and news outlets, press-release re-hosts, computed database values and chart-read values are excluded.

Claims: 890. Confirmed 694, corrected 192, unverifiable 2, refuted 2, unchecked 0. Usable: 828. Sources used: 245.

| id | verdict | statement | value | source |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| pk-identity-developer-code | confirmed | Retatrutide is an experimental medicine made by the drug company Eli Lilly. In early research it was known by the code name LY3437943. It is one chain-like molecule (a peptide) carrying a fatty-acid tail, and it acts on three hormone 'locks' (receptors): GIP, GLP-1 and glucagon. | Developer: Eli Lilly; code LY3437943; single peptide + fatty diacid; agonist at GIP, GLP-1 and glucagon (GCG) receptors | [New England Journal of Medicine / Massachusetts Medical Society](https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa2301972) |
| pk-identity-lilly-description | confirmed | Lilly describes retatrutide as an experimental once-a-week 'triple agonist': one molecule that switches on the body's receptors for three gut/pancreas hormones (GIP, GLP-1 and glucagon). In trials it is given as a weekly injection under the skin. | Investigational; once-weekly; triple hormone receptor agonist (GIP, GLP-1, glucagon); subcutaneous injection in trials | [Eli Lilly and Company](https://www.lilly.com/news/stories/what-to-know-about-retatrutide) |
| pk-structure-39-amino-acids-c20 | corrected | Retatrutide is a chain of 39 amino acids (the building blocks of proteins), arranged in a pattern based on the gut hormone GIP. Three of the 39 are lab-modified amino acids that the body does not normally use to build proteins. A 20-carbon fatty-acid 'tail' is chemically attached to one building block, a lysine at position 17. | 39 amino acids including 3 non-standard ones (Aib at positions 2 and 20; alpha-methyl-leucine at position 13); amidated C-terminus; GIP-derived backbone; C20 fatty diacid attached at Lys17 via an AEEA spacer and gamma-glutamate linker | [John Wiley & Sons (Rapid Communications in Mass Spectrometry)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13578828) |
| pk-structure-fda-substance-registry | corrected | The FDA's official substance registry lists retatrutide's 39-unit amino-acid sequence (registry code UNII NOP2Y096GV). In the simple one-letter version of the sequence, three letters actually stand for lab-modified amino acids, and a long fatty-acid side chain is hooked onto one lysine building block (the 17th). | One-letter parent sequence YAQGTFTSDYSILLDKKAQAAFIEYLLEGGPSSGAPPPS (39 residues; positions 2 and 20 are 2-methylalanine/Aib, position 13 is 2-methyl-leucine, C-terminal serinamide); UNII NOP2Y096GV; CAS 2381089-83-2; Lys17 side chain = 19-carboxy-1-oxononadecyl (C20 diacid) via gamma-Glu-AEEA linker | [U.S. FDA / NCATS Global Substance Registration System (GSRS)](https://gsrs.ncats.nih.gov/api/v1/substances(52f7c547-14ed-454d-9bc2-c9137d0b5922)?view=full) |
| pk-structure-molecular-weight | corrected (dropped: excluded host pubchem.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov) | Retatrutide's chemical formula, as listed by the World Health Organization, is C221H342N46O68. A U.S. government chemistry database (PubChem) calculates that a molecule with this formula weighs about 4,731 daltons (a unit for the mass of molecules). | Formula C221H342N46O68 (WHO INN Proposed List 128); molecular weight 4731 g/mol, computed by PubChem for CID 171390338 (record titled 'Triple G'); exact (monoisotopic) mass 4730.4784689 | [U.S. National Library of Medicine / NIH (PubChem)](https://pubchem.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/compound/171390338) |
| pk-receptor-relative-potency | confirmed | Lab tests compared retatrutide with the body's own hormones. At the GIP receptor it is about 9 times stronger than natural GIP. At the glucagon and GLP-1 receptors it is weaker than the natural hormones (about 0.3 and 0.4 times as strong). | GIPR: 8.9x more potent than native GIP; GCGR: 0.3x native glucagon; GLP-1R: 0.4x native GLP-1 | [New England Journal of Medicine / Massachusetts Medical Society](https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa2301972) |
| pk-route-once-weekly-subcutaneous | confirmed | In Lilly's big phase 3 trials, retatrutide is given once a week as a subcutaneous shot - meaning into the fatty layer just under the skin, not into a vein or muscle. | Subcutaneous, once weekly (TRIUMPH phase 3 program) | [Diabetes, Obesity and Metabolism (Wiley)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12673447) |
| pk-half-life-6-days | confirmed | 'Half-life' is how long it takes for the amount of a drug in the blood to fall by half. Retatrutide's half-life is about 6 days, which is why it is tested as a once-a-week shot. In the same study, higher dose arms gave proportionally higher blood levels ('dose proportional'). | Half-life ~6 days; dose-proportional pharmacokinetics (phase 1b, people with type 2 diabetes, 12 weeks) | [The Lancet (Elsevier)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36354040) |
| pk-blood-levels-by-dose-arm | confirmed | In a phase 2 diabetes trial, Lilly estimated each group's average 'settled' blood level of retatrutide after weekly shots. The level rose step by step with the dose arm - lowest in the 0.5 mg arm and highest in the 12 mg arm. | Average steady-state plasma concentration (Cav,ss), ng/mL: 0.5 mg arm 62.8; 4 mg arms 466 and 551; 8 mg arms 1100 and 1140; 12 mg arm 1620 | [ClinicalTrials.gov (U.S. National Library of Medicine)](https://clinicaltrials.gov/study/NCT04867785) |
| pk-tmax-single-dose-healthy | confirmed | Time to peak: after a single shot in healthy volunteers, retatrutide's blood level reached its highest point somewhere between 12 and 72 hours later (half a day to 3 days), according to a review summarizing Lilly's first human study. | Tmax 12-72 h after a single dose; mean half-life ~6 days (phase 1 single-ascending-dose study, Singapore, 45 dosed) | [Biomolecules (MDPI)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12190491) |
| pk-tmax-multiple-dose-t2d | corrected | A pharmacy-journal commentary reports that the typical (median) time to peak blood level was 12 to 49 hours after a dose, and that the half-life was about 6 days. The commentary cites Lilly's 12-week study in people with type 2 diabetes but also calls these numbers results from 'preclinical studies', so which study they came from could not be confirmed. | Median Tmax 12-49 h; half-life ~6 days (study population unclear: source says 'preclinical studies' but cites Urva et al. Lancet 2022) | [Journal of Pharmacy Technology (SAGE)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11536522) |
| pk-tmax-small-self-injection-study | confirmed | In a small study of 4 adults who were injecting retatrutide on their own (outside any Lilly trial), the drug showed up in blood within 2 hours, and blood levels usually peaked 1 to 2 days after the first shot. Peak levels varied a lot from person to person. | Detectable at 2 h in 4/4; peak typically 24-48 h; Cmax at 24 h ranged 128.6-892.3 ng/mL | [John Wiley & Sons](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13578828) |
| pk-half-life-small-study-estimate | confirmed | The same small 4-person study estimated a slightly longer half-life - about 5.5 to 9.6 days (average about 7.5 days) in the 3 people whose data fit well - than the roughly 6 days found in Lilly's trials. | Terminal half-life ~5.5-9.6 days (mean ~7.5 days), n=3 evaluable | [John Wiley & Sons](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13578828) |
| pk-steady-state-class-semaglutide | confirmed | 'Steady state' means blood levels stop climbing from week to week and settle into a repeating up-and-down pattern. For semaglutide (Ozempic), a similar once-weekly drug, this happens after 4 to 5 weekly shots. No source we could open gives retatrutide's own number. | Semaglutide steady state after 4-5 weeks of once-weekly dosing (class comparison) | [Novo Nordisk / U.S. FDA label via DailyMed](https://dailymed.nlm.nih.gov/dailymed/lookup.cfm?setid=adec4fd2-6858-4c99-91d4-531f5f2a2d79) |
| pk-steady-state-class-tirzepatide | confirmed | For tirzepatide (Zepbound/Mounjaro), Lilly's closest approved relative of retatrutide, blood levels level off after 4 weekly shots, and higher doses give proportionally higher levels. | Tirzepatide steady state after 4 weeks; dose-proportional exposure (class comparison) | [Eli Lilly / U.S. FDA label via DailyMed](https://dailymed.nlm.nih.gov/dailymed/lookup.cfm?setid=487cd7e7-434c-4925-99fa-aa80b1cc776b) |
| pk-injection-site-study-unpublished | corrected | Lilly ran a study in 85 people with higher body weight (BMI 27 to 45). It compared how much retatrutide gets into the blood when the shot goes into the thigh or upper arm instead of the belly. A second part gave the drug straight into a vein (IV) and measured blood levels and side effects. The study finished in July 2024, but no results have been posted. | NCT05959096 (Lilly J1I-MC-GZBS); Phase 1; 85 enrolled; Part A SC thigh / upper arm vs abdomen (relative bioavailability); Part B IV (safety/tolerability; AUC and Cmax as secondary outcomes); completed 2024-07-25; hasResults = false as of 2026-10-08 | [ClinicalTrials.gov (U.S. National Library of Medicine)](https://clinicaltrials.gov/study/NCT05959096) |
| pk-class-tirzepatide-sites-bioavailability | confirmed | For tirzepatide, about 80% of a shot under the skin reaches the bloodstream, and shots in the belly, thigh or upper arm gave similar total amounts of drug in the blood. (This is tirzepatide data, not retatrutide.) | Tirzepatide absolute bioavailability 80%; similar exposure abdomen/thigh/upper arm | [Eli Lilly / U.S. FDA label via DailyMed](https://dailymed.nlm.nih.gov/dailymed/lookup.cfm?setid=487cd7e7-434c-4925-99fa-aa80b1cc776b) |
| pk-class-semaglutide-sites-bioavailability | confirmed | For semaglutide (Wegovy), about 89% of a shot reaches the blood, the peak comes 1 to 3 days after the shot, and belly, thigh or upper-arm shots gave similar amounts in the blood. (Semaglutide data, not retatrutide.) | Semaglutide SC bioavailability 89%; Tmax 1-3 days; similar exposure abdomen/thigh/upper arm | [Novo Nordisk / U.S. FDA label via DailyMed](https://dailymed.nlm.nih.gov/dailymed/lookup.cfm?setid=ee06186f-2aa3-4990-a760-757579d8f77b) |
| pk-absorption-subcutaneous-space | confirmed | A 'subcutaneous' shot puts the drug into the hypodermis - the mostly-fat layer between the skin and the muscle - where it sits in the fluid between cells before it gets into the blood. | SC dose deposited in the interstitial space of the hypodermis (mainly adipose tissue) | [The AAPS Journal (Springer)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3385825) |
| pk-absorption-capillaries-vs-lymph | confirmed | From under the skin, a drug can reach the bloodstream two ways: straight into tiny blood vessels (capillaries), or the slow way through lymph vessels. Molecules up to about 16 kDa can use the capillaries; much bigger proteins mostly can't. | Size threshold ~16 kDa: <=16 kDa can enter blood capillaries; larger proteins rely on lymphatics | [The AAPS Journal (Springer)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3385825) |
| pk-absorption-lymph-threshold-primary | confirmed | The original sheep experiment behind this rule found that the bigger the molecule, the more of it travels through the lymph; molecules over about 16,000 daltons went mainly through the lymph. | MW >16,000 absorbed mainly via lymphatics (sheep study) | [Pharmaceutical Research (Springer)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2137911) |
| pk-absorption-lipidated-peptide-lymph | corrected | Even with a fatty-acid tail attached, liraglutide (a GLP-1-type drug) sent less than 0.5% of a shot through the lymph system in rats. In the same rat study, only about 10% of a liraglutide shot reached the blood at all, compared with about 100% for exenatide, which has no fatty tail. Nobody has published this test for retatrutide. | Rats, SC: lymphatic uptake <0.5% of dose for both liraglutide and exenatide; SC bioavailability ~10% (liraglutide) vs ~100% (exenatide) | [European Journal of Pharmaceutics and Biopharmaceutics (Elsevier)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/38789061) |
| pk-depot-concept-self-assembly | corrected | A 'depot' is the little pool of drug that stays under the skin after a shot and leaks into the blood slowly. For liraglutide, a GLP-1 drug with a fatty-acid tail, scientists found that the drug solution itself contains molecules stuck together in groups of seven. This may partly explain why it is absorbed slowly from under the skin. Whether retatrutide does this has not been published. | Liraglutide drug formulation contains a self-assembled heptamer that 'may partially explain' delayed SC absorption (class mechanism; based on a 2008 conference abstract) | [Frontiers in Endocrinology](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6474072) |
| pk-depot-retatrutide-profile | corrected | In the small 4-person study, people took two shots a week apart. Their blood levels of retatrutide did not fall in a smooth curve: many had a second rise about a week after the second shot. The scientists said this came mainly from the second shot adding to what was left from the first, and that it fits slow, depot-like release from under the skin. | Nonmonotonic plasma profiles with a secondary rise around 336 h (Day 14) after a second dose on Day 7; attributed to the second dose stacking on residual levels, consistent with depot-like SC absorption (n=4) | [John Wiley & Sons](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13578828) |
| pk-albumin-binding-retatrutide | corrected | The fatty-acid tail makes retatrutide grab onto albumin, a protein that floats in the blood. The grip is strong but not permanent, so the drug can let go again. While it rides on albumin, the kidneys filter less of it out and enzymes that chop up peptides break down less of it. That is why its half-life (the time it takes for half of it to leave the body) is about 6 days in people. | Reversible, noncovalent, high-affinity albumin binding (via C20 fatty diacid) -> reduced renal filtration and proteolytic degradation -> half-life approximately 6 days in humans | [John Wiley & Sons](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13578828) |
| pk-albumin-binding-tirzepatide-class | confirmed | Lilly uses the same trick in tirzepatide: a 20-carbon fatty acid that lets the drug bind albumin and stay in the body longer. About 99% of tirzepatide in blood is stuck to albumin. | Tirzepatide: C20 fatty diacid -> albumin binding; 99% albumin-bound | [Eli Lilly / U.S. FDA label via DailyMed](https://dailymed.nlm.nih.gov/dailymed/lookup.cfm?setid=487cd7e7-434c-4925-99fa-aa80b1cc776b) |
| pk-albumin-binding-kidney-semaglutide | confirmed | For semaglutide, more than 99% is bound to albumin, and the FDA label explains that this lowers how fast the kidneys clear it and protects it from being broken down. | Semaglutide >99% albumin-bound -> decreased renal clearance and protection from degradation | [Novo Nordisk / U.S. FDA label via DailyMed](https://dailymed.nlm.nih.gov/dailymed/lookup.cfm?setid=ee06186f-2aa3-4990-a760-757579d8f77b) |
| pk-native-glp1-short-half-life | confirmed | Without these tricks, the body's own GLP-1 hormone disappears fast: its half-life is about 1.5 minutes when put into a vein and about 1.5 hours when injected under the skin. That is why the drugs add a fatty-acid tail. | Native GLP-1 half-life: 1.5 min IV; 1.5 h SC | [Frontiers in Endocrinology](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6474072) |
| pk-receptor-glp1r-pancreas | confirmed | GLP-1 receptors are found on the insulin-making beta cells of the pancreas (strongly), more weakly on the pancreas cells that make digestive juices, and not on the pancreas ducts. | GLP-1R: broad in beta cells; weaker in acinar cells; absent in duct cells (human and non-human primate tissue) | [Molecular Metabolism (Elsevier)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6812410) |
| pk-receptor-glp1r-brain | corrected | In monkeys, the strongest GLP-1 receptor signal in the brain was found in parts of the hypothalamus (a brain area that helps control hunger) and in the hindbrain, including the area postrema and the nucleus of the solitary tract, plus a nerve center that sends signals to the gut (the dorsal motor nucleus of the vagus). In human brains, signs of the receptor have been found in the hypothalamus, cortex, hippocampus, thalamus and other areas. | Non-human primates: highest signal in hypothalamic nuclei, area postrema, NTS, dorsal motor nucleus of the vagus. Human: GLP-1R mRNA in cerebral cortex, hypothalamus, hippocampus, thalamus, caudate-putamen, globus pallidum | [Molecular Metabolism (Elsevier)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6812410) |
| pk-receptor-brain-access | corrected | In a study in rats and mice by Novo Nordisk scientists, semaglutide did not cross the brain's protective barrier (the blood-brain barrier). Instead it reached the brain through special spots that have no such barrier, called circumventricular organs (the area postrema is one of them), and through a few areas next to the brain's fluid-filled spaces. It reached the brainstem, the septal nucleus and the hypothalamus. Nobody has published whether retatrutide does the same. | Rodents, semaglutide only: did not cross BBB; accessed brainstem, septal nucleus, hypothalamus via circumventricular organs and select periventricular sites | [JCI Insight](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32213703) |
| pk-receptor-glp1r-stomach-not-liver | confirmed | GLP-1 receptors are found at low levels in the stomach (acid-making cells and stomach muscle) and in gut nerves - but not in the liver. | GLP-1R low in gastric parietal and smooth muscle cells, myenteric neurons; absent in liver and thyroid | [Molecular Metabolism (Elsevier)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6812410) |
| pk-receptor-glp1r-heart | corrected | Among the heart's muscle cells, GLP-1 receptors were found only in the sinoatrial node, the heart's natural pacemaker that sets the heartbeat. They were not seen in the other heart muscle cells. This comes from studies of human and monkey tissue. | GLP-1R in heart muscle cells (cardiomyocytes) restricted to the sinoatrial node; not observed in other cardiomyocytes (monkey/human tissue) | [Molecular Metabolism (Elsevier)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6812410) |
| pk-receptor-glp1-actions | corrected | What the GLP-1 receptor does when switched on: it helps the pancreas release insulin in a way that depends on blood sugar, so it releases more when blood sugar is high. It also slows how fast the stomach empties, reduces how much people eat, and makes the kidneys pass more salt and water. | GLP-1 effects: glucose-dependent insulin secretion; decreased gastric emptying; inhibition of food intake; increased natriuresis and diuresis | [Molecular Metabolism (Elsevier)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6812410) |
| pk-receptor-gipr-beta-cells | confirmed | GIP receptors sit on the pancreas's insulin-making beta cells; when switched on, they boost insulin release. | GIPR on pancreatic beta cells -> insulin secretion | [Endocrine Journal (Japan Endocrine Society)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12260194) |
| pk-receptor-gipr-organs | confirmed | GIP receptors are also found in many other places: the gut, white and brown fat tissue, the heart, the pituitary gland, part of the adrenal glands, bone, and several brain regions. | GIPR: gut, white and brown adipose tissue, heart, pituitary, adrenal cortex (inner layers), bone, several brain regions | [Endocrine Journal (Japan Endocrine Society)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12260194) |
| pk-receptor-gipr-appetite-brain | confirmed | In mice, switching on GIP-receptor nerve cells in the hypothalamus (a brain area that controls hunger) made them eat less. This is one reason GIP activity may add to appetite reduction. | Activation of hypothalamic GIPR neurons reduces food intake (mice) | [Endocrine Journal (Japan Endocrine Society)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12260194) |
| pk-receptor-gipr-fat-tissue | corrected | In lab studies using human fat cells and mice, switching on the GIP receptor in fat tissue helped store fuel after meals (when insulin is high). When insulin was low, as in fasting, it helped break stored fat down. This has not been directly shown inside people's bodies. | Fed state: GIPR agonism enhanced insulin signalling and glucose uptake; fasted / low-insulin state: GIPR agonism stimulated lipolysis (human adipocyte and mouse models) | [Endocrine Journal (Japan Endocrine Society)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12260194) |
| pk-receptor-gcgr-liver-highest | corrected | In rats, the glucagon receptor's gene message is found mostly in the liver: about 3 times more than in the kidneys and 20 times more than in fat tissue. Exact levels in humans may differ. | Rat tissue GCGR mRNA: liver ~3-fold > kidney, ~20-fold > adipose tissue (Svoboda 1994, cited by Elmendorf 2026) | [Pharmacological Research (Elsevier)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12910462) |
| pk-receptor-gcgr-heart-uncertain | corrected | Scientists still disagree on exactly where else the glucagon receptor is. Its exact location in the kidney, gut and brain is debated, and experts are not even sure the heart has it at all. | GCGR localisation in kidney, intestine, brain controversial; whether GCGR is expressed in the heart at all is debated | [Diabetologia (Springer)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13288116) |
| pk-receptor-gcgr-human-fat-conflict | confirmed | One expert review says the glucagon receptor seems to be absent from human fat tissue altogether, which conflicts with other reviews that list fat as a (minor) site. So any direct effect of glucagon on human body fat is uncertain. | GCGR appears not expressed in human adipose tissue (conflicting evidence) | [Diabetologia (Springer)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13288116) |
| pk-receptor-gcgr-liver-fat-burning | confirmed | In the liver, switching on the glucagon receptor pushes liver cells to burn fat for energy and to make less new fat - which lowers fat build-up in the liver. | GCGR activation -> increased hepatic fatty acid oxidation, decreased de novo lipogenesis | [Pharmacological Research (Elsevier)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12910462) |
| pk-receptor-gcgr-energy-expenditure-mice | confirmed | In obese mice, retatrutide caused extra weight loss because its glucagon part raised the number of calories the body burns, on top of the GIP and GLP-1 parts making them eat less. This has been shown in mice; human energy-burning results have not been published. | GCGR-mediated increase in energy expenditure added to GIPR/GLP-1R-driven intake reduction (obese mice) | [Cell Metabolism (Cell Press / Elsevier)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35985340) |
| pk-liver-fat-reduction-phase2 | confirmed | In people with fatty liver in the phase 2 obesity trial, liver fat dropped a lot within 24 weeks: by about 43% in the 1 mg arm and about 82% in the 12 mg arm, versus no change on placebo. | Relative liver-fat change at 24 wk: 1 mg -42.9%; 4 mg -57.0%; 8 mg -81.4%; 12 mg -82.4%; placebo +0.3% | [Nature Medicine (Springer Nature)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11271400) |
| pk-liver-fat-burning-marker | confirmed | A blood marker of fat burning (beta-hydroxybutyrate, a ketone) rose 2 to 3 times in the 4 mg and higher arms, which fits the idea that the glucagon part pushes the liver to burn fat. | Beta-hydroxybutyrate increased 2- to 3-fold, dose-related, at retatrutide ≥4 mg | [Nature Medicine (Springer Nature)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11271400) |
| pk-gastric-emptying-which-receptors | confirmed | Which receptors slow the stomach? GLP-1 is well known to slow stomach emptying, glucagon has been shown to do so too, but GIP does not seem to. | GLP-1: slows GE; glucagon: slows GE (rodents, humans); GIP: no apparent GE effect | [Diabetes, Obesity and Metabolism (Wiley)](https://dom-pubs.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/full/10.1111/dom.15167) |
| pk-onset-gastric-emptying-first-dose | confirmed | How fast does it act? Stomach emptying was measured about 24 hours after the first shot (day 2), and that is when the slowing was strongest. In the 3 mg-and-higher arms, the time for a test medicine to peak in the blood was delayed by about 1 hour. | Greatest GE delay after first dose (Day 2, ~24 h post-dose); acetaminophen Tmax delayed ~1 h in ≥3 mg arms; still delayed at Days 30 and 79 | [Diabetes, Obesity and Metabolism (Wiley)](https://dom-pubs.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/full/10.1111/dom.15167) |
| pk-gastric-emptying-fades | confirmed | The stomach-slowing effect got weaker with later shots - by days 30 and 79 it was fading, even in arms where the dose was still going up. The body partly gets used to this effect. | GE inhibition lower after subsequent doses; diminishing at Days 30 and 79 (tachyphylaxis) | [Diabetes, Obesity and Metabolism (Wiley)](https://dom-pubs.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/full/10.1111/dom.15167) |
| pk-onset-glucagon-hormone-24h | confirmed | In the first single-shot study, the body's own glucagon levels (fasting) dropped starting 24 hours after the shot and stayed lower up to day 15 in the 4.5 mg and 6 mg groups, according to a review of that study. | Fasting glucagon decreased from 24 h post-dose up to day 15 (4.5 and 6 mg single doses) | [Biomolecules (MDPI)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12190491) |
| pk-single-dose-weight-day43 | corrected | After a single shot in healthy volunteers, body weight was still lower than before at the study's last check-up, about 43 days (about 6 weeks) later. | Weight reduction persisted up to day 43 after a single dose (day 43 = final follow-up visit) | [Cell Metabolism (Cell Press / Elsevier)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35985340) |
| pk-glucose-hba1c-phase2-t2d | confirmed | In people with type 2 diabetes, the 12 mg arm lowered HbA1c (a 3-month average of blood sugar) by about 2 percentage points after 24 weeks, while placebo barely changed it. | HbA1c change at 24 wk: 12 mg arm -2.02%; placebo -0.01% | [The Lancet (Elsevier)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37385280) |
| pk-weight-week24-phase2 | confirmed | Weight-loss time course, part 1: in the phase 2 obesity trial, average weight loss by week 24 was about 7% (1 mg arm) up to about 17.5% (12 mg arm), versus 1.6% on placebo. | Week 24: 1 mg -7.2%; 4 mg -12.9%; 8 mg -17.3%; 12 mg -17.5%; placebo -1.6% | [New England Journal of Medicine / Massachusetts Medical Society](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37366315) |
| pk-weight-week48-phase2 | confirmed | Weight-loss time course, part 2: by week 48 the losses were bigger - about 8.7% (1 mg arm) up to 24.2% (12 mg arm), versus 2.1% on placebo. | Week 48: 1 mg -8.7%; 4 mg -17.1%; 8 mg -22.8%; 12 mg -24.2%; placebo -2.1% | [New England Journal of Medicine / Massachusetts Medical Society](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37366315) |
| pk-weight-not-plateaued-48wk | confirmed | At 48 weeks, people on retatrutide were still losing weight when the trial stopped - the weight curves had not flattened out yet. | No plateau reached at 48 weeks (phase 2 obesity) | [New England Journal of Medicine / Massachusetts Medical Society](https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa2301972) |
| pk-weight-triumph1-80wk | confirmed | In the big phase 3 trial (TRIUMPH-1, 2,339 adults with obesity, no diabetes), average weight loss after 80 weeks was 17.6% (4 mg arm), 23.7% (9 mg arm) and 25.0% (12 mg arm), versus 3.9% on placebo - counting everyone, including people who stopped the drug. | 80 wk (treatment-regimen/ITT estimand): 4 mg -17.6%; 9 mg -23.7%; 12 mg -25.0%; placebo -3.9% | [New England Journal of Medicine / Massachusetts Medical Society](https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa2604169) |
| pk-weight-triumph1-104wk | confirmed | In a 104-week extension of TRIUMPH-1 (people starting with BMI 35 or more), people kept losing weight past 80 weeks, reaching an average of about 30% weight loss at 104 weeks (2 years) in the group first assigned to 12 mg. | Extension (BMI ≥35): continued loss; up to -30.3% (85.0 lbs) at 104 weeks | [Eli Lilly and Company (via PR Newswire)](https://www.prnewswire.com/news-releases/lillys-triple-agonist-retatrutide-delivered-powerful-weight-loss-in-pivotal-phase-3-obesity-trial-302778859.html) |
| pk-heart-rate-phase2 | confirmed | Heart rate went up with retatrutide - more in higher dose arms. It peaked around week 24 and then came back down somewhat by weeks 36 and 48; the rise was similar to other GLP-1 drugs. | Dose-dependent heart-rate increase, peak at 24 weeks, decline at 36 and 48 weeks | [New England Journal of Medicine / Massachusetts Medical Society](https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa2301972) |
| pk-heart-rate-mechanism-mouse | confirmed | In lab tests on mouse heart tissue, retatrutide made the heart's pacemaker region beat faster, and this worked through the glucagon receptor. (Animal tissue study - not proven in people.) | Positive chronotropic effect in isolated mouse right atria via GCGR / cAMP / PKA | [Naunyn-Schmiedeberg's Archives of Pharmacology (Springer)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40464942) |
| pk-dropout-phase2 | confirmed | Side effects and stopping: in the phase 2 obesity trial, 6% to 16% of people on retatrutide (depending on the arm) quit treatment because of side effects, versus none on placebo. Stomach-and-gut side effects were the most common reason. | Discontinuation due to AEs: 6-16% across retatrutide arms; 0% placebo | [New England Journal of Medicine / Massachusetts Medical Society](https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa2301972) |
| pk-dropout-triumph1 | confirmed | In TRIUMPH-1, the share of people who stopped because of side effects was 4.1% (4 mg arm), 6.9% (9 mg arm) and 11.3% (12 mg arm), versus 4.9% on placebo. | AE discontinuation: 4 mg 4.1%; 9 mg 6.9%; 12 mg 11.3%; placebo 4.9% | [Eli Lilly and Company (via PR Newswire)](https://www.prnewswire.com/news-releases/lillys-triple-agonist-retatrutide-delivered-powerful-weight-loss-in-pivotal-phase-3-obesity-trial-302778859.html) |
| pk-dropout-triumph2 | confirmed | In TRIUMPH-2 (people with obesity and type 2 diabetes), the share who stopped because of side effects was 3.8% (4 mg arm), 11.6% (9 mg arm) and 7.7% (12 mg arm), versus 4.9% on placebo. | AE discontinuation: 4 mg 3.8%; 9 mg 11.6%; 12 mg 7.7%; placebo 4.9% | [Eli Lilly and Company (via PR Newswire)](https://www.prnewswire.com/news-releases/lillys-triple-agonist-retatrutide-delivered-substantial-weight-loss-and-a1c-reduction-underscoring-its-potential-promise-for-people-with-obesity-and-type-2-diabetes-302891798.html) |
| pk-dropout-triumph3 | corrected | In TRIUMPH-3 (people with severe obesity and existing heart or blood-vessel disease, called cardiovascular disease), the share who stopped because of side effects was 9.8% (9 mg arm) and 13.5% (12 mg arm), versus 4.8% on placebo. | AE discontinuation: 9 mg 9.8%; 12 mg 13.5%; placebo 4.8% (adults with BMI >=35 and established cardiovascular disease, with or without type 2 diabetes) | [Eli Lilly and Company (via PR Newswire)](https://www.prnewswire.com/news-releases/lillys-triple-agonist-retatrutide-successful-in-two-additional-phase-3-obesity-trials-delivering-significant-improvements-in-weight-and-a1c-302832674.html) |
| pk-clearance-detectable-56-days | confirmed | How long does it hang around? In the small 4-person study, retatrutide could still be measured in everyone's blood 56 days (8 weeks) after their first shot - 7 weeks after their second and last shot. | Detectable in 4/4 at day 56 after first dose (49 days after last dose): 7.1-38.4 ng/mL | [John Wiley & Sons](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13578828) |
| pk-clearance-not-in-urine | corrected | In the same 4-person study, lab tests found no retatrutide in urine at any point over 56 days. They found neither the whole molecule nor the broken-down pieces the scientists looked for, even with four different ways of preparing the samples. The authors say this suggests the kidneys pass out little of it in a form tests can catch, and that blood, not urine, is the right sample for finding it. | Not detected in urine (detection limit 2.44 ng/mL) at any time point over 56 days in 4 volunteers after two 2 mg doses; 4 sample-preparation strategies tried | [John Wiley & Sons](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13578828) |
| pk-clearance-adme-study-unpublished | corrected | Lilly ran a 'tracer' study in healthy men. Each got one shot of retatrutide tagged with a radioactive marker (carbon-14), so researchers could track where it and its breakdown pieces leave the body (urine, stool, breath). It finished in June 2023, but no results have been posted on the trial registry, and no published results paper could be found. | NCT05757531; single SC dose of [14C]-LY3437943; healthy males; sampling up to 63 days; completed 2023-06-23 (actual); hasResults = false; last update posted 2023-07-05 | [ClinicalTrials.gov (U.S. National Library of Medicine)](https://clinicaltrials.gov/study/NCT05757531) |
| pk-clearance-class-breakdown | confirmed | How are drugs like this broken down? For tirzepatide, the body chops the amino-acid chain apart (like digesting protein) and burns down the fatty-acid tail (like burning fat); whole tirzepatide is not found in urine or stool. | Tirzepatide: proteolytic cleavage, beta-oxidation of C20 diacid, amide hydrolysis; no intact drug in urine/feces | [Eli Lilly / U.S. FDA label via DailyMed](https://dailymed.nlm.nih.gov/dailymed/lookup.cfm?setid=487cd7e7-434c-4925-99fa-aa80b1cc776b) |
| pk-clearance-class-washout-semaglutide | corrected | Rule of thumb from a similar drug: semaglutide has a half-life of about 1 week, and its FDA label says it stays in the blood for about 5 to 7 weeks after the last shot. An early Lilly study in people with type 2 diabetes measured retatrutide's half-life at about 6 days, so it would likely linger for weeks too. No official 'washout' time for retatrutide has been published. | Semaglutide: half-life ~1 week; present in circulation ~5-7 weeks after last injectable dose (Wegovy label 12.3). Retatrutide: half-life ~6 days (phase 1b, type 2 diabetes; Urva et al., Lancet 2022, PMID 36354040). Retatrutide washout: inference only | [Novo Nordisk / U.S. FDA label via DailyMed](https://dailymed.nlm.nih.gov/dailymed/lookup.cfm?setid=ee06186f-2aa3-4990-a760-757579d8f77b) |
| pk-stopping-retatrutide-trial-ongoing | confirmed | What happens when people stop retatrutide? There are no published results yet. Lilly is running a trial in which, after 80 weeks on retatrutide, some people are switched to a placebo; it is expected to finish around April 2028. | NCT06859268: 80-week lead-in then 36-week randomized phase incl. switch to placebo; estimated completion 2028-04 | [ClinicalTrials.gov (U.S. National Library of Medicine)](https://clinicaltrials.gov/study/NCT06859268) |
| pk-stopping-class-tirzepatide | confirmed | Related-drug evidence (tirzepatide, not retatrutide): people who lost weight for 36 weeks and were then switched to placebo regained weight - gaining 14% over the next year - while those who kept taking it lost another 5.5%. | SURMOUNT-4: week 36 to 88 weight change -5.5% (continued tirzepatide) vs +14.0% (switched to placebo) | [JAMA (American Medical Association)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/38078870) |
| pk-stopping-class-semaglutide | confirmed | Related-drug evidence (semaglutide, not retatrutide): one year after stopping semaglutide and the lifestyle program, people had regained about two-thirds of the weight they had lost. | STEP 1 extension: ~two-thirds of prior weight loss regained 1 year after withdrawal | [Diabetes, Obesity and Metabolism (Wiley)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35441470) |
| pk-regulatory-not-approved-lilly | corrected | Retatrutide is not approved by any medicine regulator. Lilly says it is legally available only through its clinical trials. Lilly: legally available only through Lilly trials. | pk-regulatory-not-approved-tga | confirmed | Australia's medicines regulator (TGA) says retatrutide is still being tested in human trials and has not been approved by the TGA or any comparable regulator in other countries, so any product sold outside a trial could be fake. | Not approved by TGA or any comparable international regulator (14 Sept 2026) | [Therapeutic Goods Administration (Australia)](https://www.tga.gov.au/safety/safety-monitoring-and-information/safety-alerts/tga-tests-counterfeit-retatrutide-product-following-serious-adverse-event-report) |
| pk-regulatory-submission-timing | confirmed | Lilly says it plans to ask the U.S. FDA for approval (by filing a 'Biologics License Application') in the first three months of 2027. | pk-regulatory-sec-filing-wording | corrected | In its April-June 2026 results announcement, which it also filed with U.S. financial regulators (the SEC), Lilly said its clinical data for retatrutide are complete to support approvals for obesity, sleep apnea and knee arthritis pain. | trials-ph2-weight-24wk | confirmed | After 24 weeks in the phase 2 trial, average body weight had dropped by about 7% on 1 mg, 13% on 4 mg, 17% on 8 mg and 17.5% on 12 mg, compared with under 2% for people getting the dummy shot. | Least-squares mean % weight change at 24 wk: 1 mg -7.2%; 4 mg (combined) -12.9%; 8 mg (combined) -17.3%; 12 mg -17.5%; placebo -1.6% | [New England Journal of Medicine (via PubMed abstract)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37366315) |
| trials-ph2-weight-48wk | confirmed | After 48 weeks in the phase 2 trial, people on the 12 mg dose had lost about 24% of their body weight on average, versus about 2% on the dummy shot. | trials-unknown-excluded-mtc-pancreatitis | confirmed | TRIUMPH-1 did not let in people who had ever had pancreatitis (inflamed pancreas), people with a personal or family history of a rare thyroid cancer (MTC) or MEN-2 syndrome, people with diabetes, or people who had weight-loss surgery. | trials-status-bla-plan | confirmed | Lilly says it plans to ask the US FDA to approve retatrutide in early 2027. | safety-retatrutide-not-approved | confirmed | Retatrutide is still an experimental drug. | safety-gi-retatrutide-triumph2-peer-reviewed | confirmed | In the peer-reviewed TRIUMPH-2 study (adults with obesity and type 2 diabetes), diarrhea and nausea were the most common problems, and both were more common on retatrutide than on the dummy shot. | Diarrhea 27% / 34% / 34% (4/9/12 mg) vs 13% placebo; nausea 14% / 21% / 28% vs 8% | [The Lancet (Elsevier); PubMed abstract](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/42810372) |

| safety-gi-mechanism | confirmed | Why the stomach feels off: these drugs act on the brainstem's 'sickness centre' (which can cause nausea) and also slow down how fast food leaves the stomach, which can cause fullness, indigestion, reflux and bloating. | Central (brainstem area postrema / vagal) and peripheral (delayed gastric emptying) mechanisms | [Dove Medical Press (Drug Design, Development and Therapy)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13429114) |
| safety-gi-stomach-emptying-label | confirmed | The approved relative tirzepatide slows how fast the stomach empties. | safety-gi-reduce-diet | confirmed | Experts' first-line tips for nausea, indigestion and early fullness are about food, not medicine: eat smaller meals more often, choose low-fat food, skip fizzy drinks and fried or spicy food, eat slowly, stay upright for 30 minutes after eating, and don't eat within 3 hours of bedtime. | Smaller frequent meals; low-fat; avoid carbonated, fried, high-fat, spicy foods; slow eating; upright >=30 min after meals; no eating within 3 h of bedtime | [Wiley (Nutrition in Clinical Practice)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13622393) |

| safety-heart-rate-meta-analysis | corrected | A 2026 review pooled heart-rate results from trials in people with overweight or obesity who did not have diabetes. | safety-heart-rate-mechanism-glucagon | corrected | Why the heart speeds up: hormones like glucagon and GLP-1 can make the heart beat faster and squeeze harder. | safety-gallbladder-mechanism | confirmed | Why fast weight loss can cause gallstones: losing weight quickly makes bile (the digestive juice stored in the gallbladder) overloaded with cholesterol and makes the gallbladder squeeze less, so stones can form. | Rapid weight loss -> cholesterol-supersaturated bile + reduced gallbladder contraction | [Dove Medical Press (Drug Design, Development and Therapy)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13429114) |

| gap-phase3-serious-ae-rates-and-heart-rate-t1-discontinuation-why-differ | corrected | The journal and Lilly give slightly different numbers for people who stopped TRIUMPH-1 treatment because of side effects (journal table: 4.1%, 6.5% and 11.2% on 4, 9 and 12 mg vs 4.6% on placebo; Lilly: 4.1%, 6.9% and 11.3% vs 4.9%). The journal's table says its count leaves out deaths. Lilly counts from everyone who got at least one dose, and Lilly's reports for its other retatrutide trials say deaths are counted as stopping because of side effects. That probably explains most of the gap: 0, 2, 1 and 2 extra people per group in Lilly's numbers, close to but not exactly the 0, 3, 1 and 2 deaths. Neither source spells out the exact match. Both versions show about 11% stopping on 12 mg versus about 5% on placebo. | NEJM Table 3 (footnote: excludes deaths; randomized denominator stated): 24/38/65/27 = 4.1/6.5/11.2/4.6% (4/9/12 mg/placebo). Count gaps 0/2/1/2 vs deaths 0/3/1/2; exact reconciliation not published | [New England Journal of Medicine (Massachusetts Medical Society)](https://mediacenteratypon.nejmgroup-production.org/NEJMoa2604169.pdf) |

| gap-personal-history-trial-exclusions-pregnancy-stops-drug | confirmed | Across the TRIUMPH program, a participant who became pregnant would normally stop the study drug. Pregnancy was the one reason for stopping after which people were not asked to stay in the study. | Pregnancy listed as clinical consideration for discontinuation | [Diabetes, Obesity and Metabolism (Wiley), via Europe PMC (PMC12673447)](https://doi.org/10.1111/dom.70209) |
| gap-personal-history-trial-exclusions-pregnancy-testing | corrected | In TRIUMPH-2 and TRIUMPH-3, women who could become pregnant and were having sex with men needed a negative blood pregnancy test at screening and another negative urine test within 24 hours before their first dose of the study drug. | Negative serum test at screening + negative urine test within 24 h before first exposure (WOCBP not abstinent / not in same-sex relationship; TRIUMPH-2 and -3) | [Eli Lilly and Company, published via EMA CTIS](https://euclinicaltrials.eu/ctis-public/view/2023-503658-11-00) |
| gap-personal-history-trial-exclusions-contraception-two-methods | confirmed | In TRIUMPH-2 and TRIUMPH-3, women who could get pregnant and were sexually active with men had to use two kinds of birth control at the same time, and at least one had to be a 'highly effective' kind. They had to keep this up during the study and for at least 60 days after their last dose. | 2 methods, ≥1 highly effective; continued ≥60 days after last dose | [Eli Lilly and Company, published via EMA CTIS](https://euclinicaltrials.eu/ctis-public/view/2023-503658-11-00) |
| gap-personal-history-trial-exclusions-oral-contraceptive-not-alone | confirmed | The birth control pill (combined pill or progestin-only 'mini-pill') counted as a 'highly effective' method in TRIUMPH-2 and TRIUMPH-3. Because two methods were required, though, the pill alone was not enough: a second method such as condoms with spermicide was also needed. | Oral contraceptive = highly effective method, but a second method was required | [Eli Lilly and Company, published via EMA CTIS](https://euclinicaltrials.eu/ctis-public/view/2023-503658-11-00) |
| gap-personal-history-trial-exclusions-patch-weight-limit | confirmed | The skin birth-control patch only counted as 'highly effective' in these trials for women weighing less than 90 kg (198 pounds). | Patch counted only for women <198 lb / 90 kg | [Eli Lilly and Company, published via EMA CTIS](https://euclinicaltrials.eu/ctis-public/view/2023-503658-11-00) |
| gap-personal-history-trial-exclusions-recent-injectable-implant-contraceptive | confirmed | Women who had started a birth-control shot or implant (such as Depo-Provera or Nexplanon) in the 18 months before joining were kept out of TRIUMPH-2 and TRIUMPH-3. Women with an IUD were fine. | Started injectable/implantable contraceptive within 18 months excluded; IUDs acceptable | [Eli Lilly and Company, published via EMA CTIS](https://euclinicaltrials.eu/ctis-public/view/2023-503658-11-00) |
| gap-personal-history-trial-exclusions-men-contraception-sperm | confirmed | Men in TRIUMPH-2 and TRIUMPH-3 were asked not to donate sperm during the study and for 4 months after their last dose. Men with partners who could get pregnant had to use contraception, including condoms, over the same period. | No sperm donation and contraception for 4 months after last dose | [Eli Lilly and Company, published via EMA CTIS](https://euclinicaltrials.eu/ctis-public/view/2023-503658-11-00) |
| gap-personal-history-trial-exclusions-triumph1-no-diabetes | confirmed | TRIUMPH-1 (the main obesity trial) did not let in anyone with diabetes of any type. | Diabetes mellitus excluded (TRIUMPH-1) | [ClinicalTrials.gov (U.S. National Library of Medicine)](https://clinicaltrials.gov/study/NCT05929066) |
| gap-personal-history-trial-exclusions-triumph2-diabetes-range | confirmed | TRIUMPH-2 only included people with type 2 diabetes whose long-term blood sugar (HbA1c) was between 6.5% and 10.5%. They had to be on a steady diabetes treatment (diet/exercise or up to three diabetes pills) for at least 90 days. | HbA1c 6.5–10.5%; stable ≥90 days on diet/exercise or ≤3 oral glucose-lowering medicines | [The Lancet (via PubMed, PMID 42810372)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/42810372) |
| gap-personal-history-trial-exclusions-triumph2-no-insulin-hypos | confirmed | TRIUMPH-2 kept out people who had used insulin (except briefly) or GLP-1-type drugs in the 3 months before joining. It also kept out anyone who had a severe low-blood-sugar episode, or couldn't feel low blood sugar coming on, in the past 6 months. | Insulin/DPP-4i/amylin/GLP-1/GIP-GLP-1 within 90 days excluded; severe hypoglycemia or unawareness ≤6 months excluded | [Eli Lilly and Company, published via EMA CTIS](https://euclinicaltrials.eu/ctis-public/view/2023-503658-11-00) |
| gap-personal-history-trial-exclusions-triumph3-t2d-and-insulin-allowed | confirmed | TRIUMPH-3 (people with heart disease) allowed people with or without type 2 diabetes, and people with diabetes could stay on insulin. TRIUMPH-2 did not allow ongoing background insulin. | TRIUMPH-3: T2D allowed (HbA1c 6.5–10.5%, ≤3 oral agents) and background insulin allowed; TRIUMPH-2: no background maintenance insulin | [Diabetes, Obesity and Metabolism (Wiley), via Europe PMC](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC12673447/supplementaryFiles) |
| gap-personal-history-trial-exclusions-recent-glp1-users | confirmed | People who had used a GLP-1 drug (like semaglutide) or a GIP/GLP-1 drug (like tirzepatide) in the 3 months before joining were kept out of all TRIUMPH trials. Switching straight from those drugs to retatrutide was not studied. | GLP-1R or GIP/GLP-1R agonist within 90 days excluded (TRIUMPH-1, -2, -3, -4) | [Diabetes, Obesity and Metabolism (Wiley), via Europe PMC](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC12673447/supplementaryFiles) |
| gap-personal-history-trial-exclusions-liver-disease | confirmed | People with hepatitis or other liver disease were kept out of all TRIUMPH trials. People with fatty liver disease could join if a key liver blood test (ALT) was less than 3 times the normal upper limit. | Acute/chronic hepatitis or other liver disease excluded; NAFLD allowed if ALT <3.0x ULN | [Diabetes, Obesity and Metabolism (Wiley), via Europe PMC](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC12673447/supplementaryFiles) |
| gap-personal-history-trial-exclusions-cancer-5y | confirmed | People diagnosed with cancer in the 5 years before joining were kept out of all TRIUMPH trials. The exceptions were some removed skin cancers and very early (stage 0) cervical cancer. | Malignancy within 5 years excluded (exceptions: resected basal/squamous cell skin cancer; stage 0 cervical cancer) | [Diabetes, Obesity and Metabolism (Wiley), via Europe PMC](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC12673447/supplementaryFiles) |
| gap-personal-history-trial-exclusions-thyroid-tsh | confirmed | People whose thyroid blood test (TSH) was out of the normal range were kept out of the trials. People with an underactive thyroid that was well controlled on a steady thyroid medicine for at least 2 months could join. | TSH outside central-lab range excluded; stable thyroid replacement ≥2 months allowed | [Eli Lilly and Company, published via EMA CTIS](https://euclinicaltrials.eu/ctis-public/view/2023-503658-11-00) |
| gap-personal-history-trial-exclusions-blood-disorders-hba1c | confirmed | In TRIUMPH-2 and TRIUMPH-3, people with blood conditions that can throw off the HbA1c blood-sugar test, such as sickle cell disease or hemolytic anemia (red blood cells breaking down too early), were kept out. | Hematological condition interfering with HbA1c excluded | [Eli Lilly and Company, published via EMA CTIS](https://euclinicaltrials.eu/ctis-public/view/2023-503658-11-00) |
| gap-personal-history-trial-exclusions-organ-transplant | confirmed | People who had an organ transplant, or were waiting for one, were kept out of TRIUMPH-2 and TRIUMPH-3. Cornea (eye surface) transplants were allowed. | Organ transplant or awaiting transplant excluded; corneal transplant allowed | [Eli Lilly and Company, published via EMA CTIS](https://euclinicaltrials.eu/ctis-public/view/2023-503658-11-00) |
| gap-personal-history-trial-exclusions-autoimmune-steroids | confirmed | People taking long-term steroid pills or injections, or with an active autoimmune disease (like lupus or rheumatoid arthritis) likely to need them, were kept out. Steroid creams, eye drops, nasal sprays and inhalers were fine. | Chronic systemic glucocorticoids >14 days or within 90 days excluded; topical/intra-ocular/intranasal/inhaled allowed | [Eli Lilly and Company, published via EMA CTIS](https://euclinicaltrials.eu/ctis-public/view/2023-503658-11-00) |
| gap-personal-history-trial-exclusions-eating-disorder-alcohol-drugs | confirmed | Study doctors could turn away people with conditions that might make the trial unsafe for them. The trial plans named recreational drug use, alcohol abuse and a diagnosed eating disorder as examples. | Investigator-discretion exclusion; examples include eating disorder, alcohol abuse, recreational drug use | [Eli Lilly and Company, published via EMA CTIS](https://euclinicaltrials.eu/ctis-public/view/2023-503658-11-00) |
| gap-personal-history-trial-exclusions-planned-surgery | confirmed | People who already had a planned surgery (other than minor procedures) scheduled during the study were kept out of TRIUMPH-2 and TRIUMPH-3. | Planned non-minor surgery during study excluded | [Eli Lilly and Company, published via EMA CTIS](https://euclinicaltrials.eu/ctis-public/view/2023-503658-11-00) |
| gap-personal-history-trial-exclusions-triumph3-surgery-registry-conflict | confirmed | Two official records describe TRIUMPH-3's weight-loss-surgery rule differently. The EU registry summary says surgery within the past year; the full trial plan says any past or planned weight-loss surgery. | EU CTIS summary: within 1 year; protocol: prior or planned (cosmetic liposuction/abdominoplasty >1 year allowed) | [EMA Clinical Trials Information System (CTIS)](https://euclinicaltrials.eu/ctis-public/view/2023-503659-88-00) |
| gap-personal-history-trial-exclusions-triumph1-full-list-not-public | confirmed (dropped: researcher could not open the source text) | For TRIUMPH-1, only the 'key' rules are public. We could not confirm its exact rules on the depression questionnaire score, suicide-risk questions, slow stomach emptying (gastroparesis), or birth control, though TRIUMPH-2 and TRIUMPH-3 had such rules. | TRIUMPH-1 full eligibility text not accessible | [Diabetes, Obesity and Metabolism (Wiley), via Europe PMC (PMC12673447)](https://doi.org/10.1111/dom.70209) |
| gap-surgery-pregnancy-practical-guidance-ms2024-who-wrote-it | confirmed | In late 2024, five medical groups published one shared set of advice on GLP-1 drugs around surgery, scopes (like endoscopy) and sedation: gut doctors (AGA), weight-loss surgeons (ASMBS), anesthesia doctors (ASA), an obesity-surgery care society (ISPCOP) and endoscopic surgeons (SAGES). | Published 29 Oct 2024, identical text in Surg Obes Relat Dis, Clin Gastroenterol Hepatol and Surg Endosc | [Surgical Endoscopy (Springer) on behalf of AGA, ASMBS, ASA, ISPCOP, SAGES](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11666732) |
| gap-surgery-pregnancy-practical-guidance-ms2024-shared-decision | confirmed | The main rule in the 2024 advice: whether to keep taking a GLP-1 drug before a procedure is a shared decision between the patient, the surgery or scope team, the anesthesia team and whoever prescribed the drug. | Recommendation 1 (shared decision-making) | [Surgical Endoscopy (Springer) on behalf of AGA, ASMBS, ASA, ISPCOP, SAGES](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11666732) |
| gap-surgery-pregnancy-practical-guidance-ms2024-risk-escalation | confirmed | The groups say the risk of food staying in the stomach is higher while someone is still in the 'ramp-up' phase of a GLP-1 drug than once they are on a steady dose. | Escalation phase listed as risk factor #1 | [Surgical Endoscopy (Springer) on behalf of AGA, ASMBS, ASA, ISPCOP, SAGES](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11666732) |
| gap-surgery-pregnancy-practical-guidance-ms2024-risk-weekly | confirmed | Weekly injected drugs count as a higher-risk feature in the 2024 advice, because stomach side effects happen more often with weekly drugs than with daily ones. Retatrutide was developed as a once-a-week injection. | Weekly dosing listed as risk factor #3 | [Surgical Endoscopy (Springer) on behalf of AGA, ASMBS, ASA, ISPCOP, SAGES](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11666732) |
| gap-surgery-pregnancy-practical-guidance-ms2024-risk-symptoms | confirmed | Feeling sick, throwing up, belly pain, indigestion or constipation can be signs that the stomach is emptying slowly, and the 2024 advice counts them as raising the risk. Other conditions that slow the gut, such as gastroparesis or Parkinson's disease, count too. | Risk factors #4 (GI symptoms) and #5 (other conditions that slow gastric emptying) | [Surgical Endoscopy (Springer) on behalf of AGA, ASMBS, ASA, ISPCOP, SAGES](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11666732) |
| gap-surgery-pregnancy-practical-guidance-ms2024-continue-if-low-risk | confirmed | If none of those higher-risk features apply, the 2024 advice says the drug can usually be continued before surgery. | Recommendation 1b | [Surgical Endoscopy (Springer) on behalf of AGA, ASMBS, ASA, ISPCOP, SAGES](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11666732) |
| gap-surgery-pregnancy-practical-guidance-ms2024-hold-duration-unknown | confirmed | If the team decides the drug should be paused, the 2024 advice says nobody knows exactly how long that pause needs to be. | Hold duration: unknown | [Surgical Endoscopy (Springer) on behalf of AGA, ASMBS, ASA, ISPCOP, SAGES](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11666732) |
| gap-surgery-pregnancy-practical-guidance-ms2024-fallback-one-week | confirmed | When a pause is chosen, the 2024 advice falls back on the older anesthesia rule: about one week for weekly drugs and the day of surgery for daily drugs. Later expert groups (below) say one week is probably too short for long-acting drugs. | Weekly formulations: held 1 week before surgery; daily formulations: held day of surgery | [Surgical Endoscopy (Springer) on behalf of AGA, ASMBS, ASA, ISPCOP, SAGES](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11666732) |
| gap-surgery-pregnancy-practical-guidance-ms2024-24h-liquid-diet | confirmed | Instead of, or as well as, pausing the drug, the 2024 advice suggests a liquids-only diet for at least 24 hours before the procedure when the stomach may be emptying slowly. Doctors already use the same idea before colonoscopies and weight-loss surgery. | Liquid diet for at least 24 h before the procedure | [Surgical Endoscopy (Springer) on behalf of AGA, ASMBS, ASA, ISPCOP, SAGES](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11666732) |
| gap-surgery-pregnancy-practical-guidance-ms2024-day-of-check | confirmed | Even with a plan in place, the team should check on the day of the procedure for signs that the stomach is still full. | Day-of-procedure symptom assessment for all patients | [Surgical Endoscopy (Springer) on behalf of AGA, ASMBS, ASA, ISPCOP, SAGES](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11666732) |
| gap-surgery-pregnancy-practical-guidance-ms2024-change-plan-or-cancel | confirmed | If the team thinks food may still be in the stomach, they may switch to a faster way of putting the patient to sleep and placing a breathing tube to protect the lungs, or they may postpone the procedure. | Rapid sequence induction vs procedure cancellation | [Surgical Endoscopy (Springer) on behalf of AGA, ASMBS, ASA, ISPCOP, SAGES](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11666732) |
| gap-surgery-pregnancy-practical-guidance-ms2024-not-for-triple-agonists | confirmed | The 2024 advice itself says it may need to change for newer 'dual' and 'triple' drugs. Retatrutide is a triple agonist, so this advice was not written or tested with retatrutide in mind. | Authors flag need for modification for dual and triple agonists | [Surgical Endoscopy (Springer) on behalf of AGA, ASMBS, ASA, ISPCOP, SAGES](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11666732) |
| gap-surgery-pregnancy-practical-guidance-asa2023-original-rule | confirmed | In 2023, the American Society of Anesthesiologists first suggested pausing weekly GLP-1 drugs for a week before planned surgery. This was the first widely used rule. | 2023 ASA: consider holding weekly GLP-1 RAs 1 week before elective procedure/surgery | [American Society of Anesthesiologists](https://www.asahq.org/about-asa/newsroom/news-releases/2023/06/american-society-of-anesthesiologists-consensus-based-guidance-on-preoperative) |
| gap-surgery-pregnancy-practical-guidance-asa2023-superseded | corrected | The anesthesia society's 2023 page now carries a note pointing to the newer 2024 multi-society advice, for which ASA issued an 'Affirmation of Value' (a formal show of support, not co-authorship). That newer advice makes continuing the drug the default for lower-risk patients. It keeps the 2023 one-week pause only as a fallback for when the team decides a pause is needed. | ASA 'Affirmation of Value' for the multi-society document: October 2024 per the ASA page; the document itself says the ASA Administrative Council affirmed its value in May 2024 | [American Society of Anesthesiologists](https://www.asahq.org/about-asa/newsroom/news-releases/2023/06/american-society-of-anesthesiologists-consensus-based-guidance-on-preoperative) |
| gap-surgery-pregnancy-practical-guidance-spaqi2025-continue | confirmed | A 2025 expert panel led by a perioperative-care society (SPAQI) recommends keeping GLP-1 drugs going around surgery for people without serious stomach symptoms. Serious means bad nausea, vomiting or being unable to keep food down; just feeling full does not count. | Recommendation 1 (grade B) | [British Journal of Anaesthesia (Elsevier)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12597468) |
| gap-surgery-pregnancy-practical-guidance-spaqi2025-24h-solids | confirmed | The SPAQI panel recommends no solid food for 24 hours before anesthesia, with only clear liquids allowed, for people on these drugs who don't have serious stomach symptoms. | 24 h solid fast, clear liquids only (Recommendation 2, grade B) | [British Journal of Anaesthesia (Elsevier)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12597468) |
| gap-surgery-pregnancy-practical-guidance-spaqi2025-clear-drink-timing | confirmed | SPAQI also advises stopping sugary clear drinks (10% sugar or more) 8 hours before anesthesia, and other clear drinks 4 hours before. This is longer than the usual 2 hours. | 8 h for clear liquids with >=10% glucose (Rec 3, grade C); 4 h for clear liquids with <10% glucose (Rec 4, grade C) | [British Journal of Anaesthesia (Elsevier)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12597468) |
| gap-surgery-pregnancy-practical-guidance-spaqi2025-symptoms-refer | confirmed | People with serious stomach symptoms should go back to their prescriber to adjust their diet and medicine before a planned procedure under anesthesia, according to SPAQI. | Recommendation 5 (grade E) | [British Journal of Anaesthesia (Elsevier)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12597468) |
| gap-surgery-pregnancy-practical-guidance-spaqi2025-four-to-five-half-lives | confirmed | The stomach-slowing effect only wears off after the drug has been stopped for about 4 to 5 'half-lives'. A half-life is the time it takes for half of the drug to leave the body. For long-acting weekly drugs that means several weeks, which is why short pauses don't fully work. | 4-5 half-lives; 'several weeks' for dulaglutide, semaglutide, tirzepatide | [British Journal of Anaesthesia (Elsevier)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12597468) |
| gap-surgery-pregnancy-practical-guidance-spaqi2025-first-4-weeks | confirmed | Stomach slowing and side effects are most common in roughly the first 4 weeks after starting a long-acting GLP-1 drug or moving up to a higher dose. | ~4 weeks after initiation or dose escalation | [British Journal of Anaesthesia (Elsevier)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12597468) |
| gap-surgery-pregnancy-practical-guidance-spaqi2025-full-stomach-4-weeks | confirmed | If no stomach ultrasound is done, SPAQI says anyone who has taken a long-acting GLP-1 drug in the past 4 weeks should be treated by the anesthesia team as if their stomach is full. | Long-acting GLP-1 RA within past 4 weeks = treat as 'full stomach' (absent ultrasound) | [British Journal of Anaesthesia (Elsevier)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12597468) |
| gap-surgery-pregnancy-practical-guidance-spaqi2025-semaglutide-pause-data | confirmed | In one study of semaglutide users, the stomach looked like that of non-users only after the drug had been paused for more than 14 days in people without symptoms, or more than 21 days in people with symptoms. | >14 days (no symptoms); >21 days (with symptoms) | [British Journal of Anaesthesia (Elsevier)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12597468) |
| gap-surgery-pregnancy-practical-guidance-spaqi2025-liquid-vs-clear | confirmed | Expert groups don't fully agree on the pre-procedure diet. The 2024 multi-society advice says 'liquid diet', while SPAQI says it should be clear liquids only, because some non-clear liquids are high in fat or calories. | Disagreement: 'liquid diet' (2024 multi-society) vs 'clear liquid diet' (SPAQI 2025; ANZ 2025 'clear fluid') | [British Journal of Anaesthesia (Elsevier)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12597468) |
| gap-surgery-pregnancy-practical-guidance-uk2025-continue | confirmed | UK anaesthesia, diabetes, obesity-surgery and pharmacy groups (2025) say people should keep taking GLP-1 and dual GIP/GLP-1 drugs around surgery, and the team should manage the inhaling-stomach-contents risk in other ways. | Box 1: continue throughout the peri-operative period | [Anaesthesia (Wiley), for the Association of Anaesthetists, ABCD, BOMSS, CPOC, JBDS-IP, RCoA, SOBA, UKCPA](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11885194) |
| gap-surgery-pregnancy-practical-guidance-uk2025-one-week-not-enough | confirmed | The UK statement says a one-week pause still leaves a high chance of food in the stomach, even in people who fasted properly. Newer data suggest that even up to three weeks off may not be enough. | 1 week of cessation: high risk persists; up to 3 weeks: increased gastric volume still reported | [Anaesthesia (Wiley), for the Association of Anaesthetists et al.](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11885194) |
| gap-surgery-pregnancy-practical-guidance-anz2025-24h-clear-fluid | confirmed | Australian and New Zealand diabetes, anaesthesia, gut and obesity groups (2025) recommend 24 hours of clear fluids only, followed by the usual 6-hour fast, for everyone taking these drugs before a procedure. They do not recommend stopping the drug for a planned procedure. | 24 h clear fluid diet + standard 6 h fast; elective cessation not recommended | [Anaesthesia and Intensive Care (SAGE), for ADS, ANZCA, GESA, NACOS](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12420935) |
| gap-surgery-pregnancy-practical-guidance-anz2025-1-2-weeks-useless | confirmed | According to the Australian and New Zealand groups, skipping 1 to 2 weeks of a long-acting drug like semaglutide or tirzepatide is unlikely to undo the stomach slowing. | 1-2 week omission unlikely to alter slowed gastric emptying | [Anaesthesia and Intensive Care (SAGE)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12420935) |
| gap-surgery-pregnancy-practical-guidance-anz2025-no-safe-stop-period | confirmed | The Australian and New Zealand groups also say no one can yet name a stopping period that guarantees the stomach is back to normal. | No adequate cessation period can be recommended | [Anaesthesia and Intensive Care (SAGE)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12420935) |
| gap-surgery-pregnancy-practical-guidance-anz2025-full-stomach | confirmed | Without a 24-hour fluids-only diet or a check of the stomach (ultrasound or a thin camera), everyone on these drugs should be treated as if they have not fasted. | Key point 6: treat as non-fasted/full stomach | [Anaesthesia and Intensive Care (SAGE)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12420935) |
| gap-surgery-pregnancy-practical-guidance-ada2026-one-week-insufficient | confirmed | The American Diabetes Association's 2026 care standards say one week off a once-weekly drug is probably not enough to remove the risk, and the right length of pause is unknown. | 1-week hold of once-weekly agent 'likely insufficient'; required duration unknown | [American Diabetes Association Professional Practice Committee / Diabetes Care](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12690180) |
| gap-surgery-pregnancy-practical-guidance-ada2026-30-days | confirmed | One study found that people who had paused semaglutide for up to 30 days (10 days on average) still tended to have more food left in their stomach. | Up to 30 days held (mean 10 days): still increased residual gastric content | [American Diabetes Association / Diabetes Care](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12690180) |
| gap-surgery-pregnancy-practical-guidance-ada2026-personalized | confirmed | The ADA recommends a personalized plan for each person rather than one fixed rule. The plan weighs why the drug is taken, dose, how long the person has been on it, stomach symptoms, the type of procedure and the type of anesthesia. A 24-hour liquid diet beforehand 'may be helpful'. | Personalized approach recommended; 24 h liquid nutrition protocol 'may be helpful' | [American Diabetes Association / Diabetes Care](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12690180) |
| gap-surgery-pregnancy-practical-guidance-fda-label-aspiration-reports | confirmed | The US FDA label for tirzepatide (Zepbound) warns of rare cases where people on GLP-1 drugs breathed stomach contents into their lungs during anesthesia or deep sedation, even though they said they had fasted as instructed. | Warnings & Precautions 5.9 (Zepbound label revised 08/2026) | [US FDA / DailyMed (Eli Lilly and Company)](https://dailymed.nlm.nih.gov/dailymed/lookup.cfm?setid=487cd7e7-434c-4925-99fa-aa80b1cc776b) |
| gap-surgery-pregnancy-practical-guidance-fda-label-insufficient-data | confirmed | The FDA label says there isn't enough data to know whether changing fasting rules or pausing the drug lowers this risk. It tells patients to inform their healthcare providers before any planned surgery or procedure. | No FDA-recommended hold period or fasting change | [US FDA / DailyMed (Eli Lilly and Company)](https://dailymed.nlm.nih.gov/dailymed/lookup.cfm?setid=487cd7e7-434c-4925-99fa-aa80b1cc776b) |
| gap-surgery-pregnancy-practical-guidance-mhra-dsu-no-pause-time | confirmed | European and UK regulators looked at whether they could set a specific pause time before anaesthesia, or new fasting rules. They decided the evidence was too limited and that anaesthetists should judge each case. | No regulator-set pause time; individualised assessment | [MHRA (UK Medicines and Healthcare products Regulatory Agency)](https://www.gov.uk/drug-safety-update/glp-1-and-dual-gip-slash-glp-1-receptor-agonists-potential-risk-of-pulmonary-aspiration-during-general-anaesthesia-or-deep-sedation) |
| gap-surgery-pregnancy-practical-guidance-mhra-smpc-no-withholding | confirmed | In the UK, the official product information for every licensed GLP-1 and dual GIP/GLP-1 drug gives no instruction to stop the drug before surgery or sedation. | UK SmPCs: no withholding guidance | [MHRA](https://www.gov.uk/drug-safety-update/glp-1-and-dual-gip-slash-glp-1-receptor-agonists-potential-risk-of-pulmonary-aspiration-during-general-anaesthesia-or-deep-sedation) |
| gap-surgery-pregnancy-practical-guidance-mhra-may-not-disclose | confirmed | UK regulators warn doctors that people who bought these drugs for cosmetic weight loss often don't mention them unless they are asked directly. | gap-surgery-pregnancy-practical-guidance-mhra-public-tell-team | confirmed | The UK regulator's public advice (updated February 2026) says to tell your whole team, including the anaesthetist, before surgery. | gap-surgery-pregnancy-practical-guidance-reta-half-life-6-days | confirmed | In an early Lilly study in people with type 2 diabetes, retatrutide's half-life was about 6 days. That means it takes roughly 6 days for the amount of drug in the blood to fall by half. | ~6 days | [The Lancet (Elsevier); Eli Lilly-funded](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36354040) |
| gap-surgery-pregnancy-practical-guidance-reta-half-life-range | confirmed | Lilly's trial protocol reports that, across single doses in the first human study, retatrutide's average half-life ranged from about 5 to 7 days. | Mean terminal half-life ~5 to 7 days (Phase 1 single-dose study) | [Eli Lilly and Company (protocol posted on ClinicalTrials.gov)](https://cdn.clinicaltrials.gov/large-docs/60/NCT04881760/Prot_000.pdf) |
| gap-surgery-pregnancy-practical-guidance-reta-effect-day-43 | confirmed | In the first human study, a single injection of retatrutide still showed weight loss 43 days later. Its effects can outlast one injection by weeks. | Weight reduction persisted to day 43 after a single dose | [Cell Metabolism (Elsevier); Eli Lilly authors](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35985340) |
| gap-surgery-pregnancy-practical-guidance-reta-detectable-56-days | confirmed | In a small anti-doping lab study, retatrutide could still be detected in the blood about 56 days after the first injection. | ~56 days plasma detection window | [Rapid Communications in Mass Spectrometry (Wiley)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/42745421) |
| gap-surgery-pregnancy-practical-guidance-reta-delays-gastric-emptying | confirmed | Retatrutide does slow stomach emptying, like other GLP-1 drugs. In a Lilly drug study the effect was strongest early in treatment. | Delayed gastric emptying by acetaminophen absorption; greatest early in exposure | [Therapeutics and Clinical Risk Management (Dove Medical Press)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13523872) |
| gap-surgery-pregnancy-practical-guidance-reta-no-cessation-interval | confirmed | Retatrutide has no approved label and no established 'stop before surgery' period. Its stomach-slowing effect has been shown, but there are no data on stomach contents or breathing-in events around surgery. | No approved labeling; no established perioperative cessation interval | [Therapeutics and Clinical Risk Management (Dove Medical Press)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13523872) |
| gap-surgery-pregnancy-practical-guidance-pk-cannot-prove-stomach-normal | confirmed | Knowing how long a drug stays in the blood helps estimate exposure after a pause, but it can't prove the stomach is back to normal or that the risk is gone. | PK estimates exposure but cannot confirm gastric recovery | [Therapeutics and Clinical Risk Management (Dove Medical Press)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13523872) |
| gap-surgery-pregnancy-practical-guidance-inference-surgery-math | corrected | OUR ESTIMATE, NOT AN OFFICIAL RULE: one expert panel (SPAQI) says pauses shorter than about 3 to 5 half-lives may not let the stomach fully recover; in another place it says 4 to 5. With retatrutide's half-life of about 6 days, 3 to 5 half-lives is about 18 to 30 days (roughly 2.5 to 4.3 weeks). No study has tested this for retatrutide, and blood-level math can't prove the stomach is back to normal. Several expert groups (the US multi-society, SPAQI, ANZ and ADA) now favor telling the team plus a 24-hour liquid or clear-liquid diet over long pauses. | Inference: 3-5 x ~6 days = ~18-30 days (~2.6-4.3 weeks); 4-5 x ~6 days = ~24-30 days (~3.4-4.3 weeks) | [British Journal of Anaesthesia (Elsevier)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12597468) |
